Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a:b (where length = x₂-x₁, width = y₂-y₁)
   - Is as close as possible to the center of the original rectangle (minimizing Euclidean distance from (x,y) to the center)
   - If ties, choose lexicographically smallest (x₁,y₁,x₂,y₂)

## Key Insights

1. The ratio condition: (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b
   - This means: b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)
   - So: x₂-x₁ = (a/b) × (y₂-y₁)

2. Since x₁,y₁,x₂,y₂ must be integers, we need to find integer solutions to the ratio condition.

3. We can express the width (w = y₂-y₁) and length (l = x₂-x₁) such that l/w = a/b.
   - This means l = (a/g) × k and w = (b/g) × k for some integer k and gcd(a,b) = g.

4. For a given width w, the length l must be (a×w)/b, so w must be divisible by b.

5. We need to find valid ranges:
   - x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ → x₂ - x₁ ≥ 0 and x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂
   - y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ → y₂ - y₁ ≥ 0 and y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂

## Solution Strategy

1. For each possible width w (multiple of b), calculate the corresponding length l = (a×w)/b
2. For each valid width w, find valid ranges for x₁,x₂,y₁,y₂
3. Check if the rectangle contains (x,y)
4. Among all valid rectangles, find the one closest to (x,y) in Euclidean distance
5. If tie, choose lexicographically smallest

## Implementation

```python
import math
from fractions import Fraction

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # We need to find rectangles where (x2-x1)/(y2-y1) = a/b
    # This means (x2-x1) = (a/b) * (y2-y1)
    # So (x2-x1) * b = (y2-y1) * a
    # Let g = gcd(a,b), then a = a/g * g, b = b/g * g
    # So b * (x2-x1) = a * (y2-y1)
    
    g = gcd(a, b)
    a_reduced = a // g
    b_reduced = b // g
    
    best_rect = None
    min_dist_squared = float('inf')
    
    # We'll iterate over possible widths (y2-y1) that are multiples of b_reduced
    # Width must be in [1, m] and must be a multiple of b_reduced
    
    # For each multiple of b_reduced as width, calculate corresponding length
    max_width = m
    max_length = n
    
    # We need to find valid ranges
    # y2 = y1 + w, y1 in [0, m-w] and y1 <= y <= y2
    # x2 = x1 + l, x1 in [0, n-l] and x1 <= x <= x2
    
    # For width w (multiple of b_reduced), length l = (a_reduced * w) / b_reduced
    # l must be integer, so w must be multiple of b_reduced
    
    for w in range(b_reduced, max_width + 1, b_reduced):
        l = (a_reduced * w) // b_reduced
        
        if l > max_length:
            break
            
        # For this width w, we need to find valid height and length
        # y1 can range from 0 to m-w
        # y1 <= y <= y1 + w
        # For y1 to contain y: y1 <= y <= y1 + w
        # This gives: y - w <= y1 <= y
        
        y1_min = max(0, y - w)
        y1_max = min(m - w, y)
        
        if y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # For x1: x1 <= x <= x1 + l
        # This gives: x - l <= x1 <= x
        x1_min = max(0, x - l)
        x1_max = min(n - l, x)
        
        if x1_min > x1_max:
            continue
            
        # We have valid ranges for x1 and y1
        # For each x1 in [x1_min, x1_max] and each y1 in [y1_min, y1_max]
        # we get a valid rectangle (x1, y1, x1+l, y1+w)
        
        # To minimize Euclidean distance to center (x,y), where center is ((x1+l)/2, (y1+w)/2)
        # Distance squared = (x - (x1+l)/2)² + (y - (y1+w)/2)²
        
        # For fixed w, we can find optimal x1 and y1:
        # For x1: minimize |x - (x1+l)/2| = |2x - (x1+l)| / 2
        # This is minimized when x1 is closest to (2x - l)
        
        x1_target = 2 * x - l
        best_x1 = x1_min
        if x1_target >= x1_min and x1_target <= x1_max:
            best_x1 = int(x1_target)
        else:
            # Choose the closest endpoint
            if x1_target < x1_min:
                best_x1 = x1_min
            else:
                best_x1 = x1_max
        
        y1_target = 2 * y - w
        best_y1 = y1_min
        if y1_target >= y1_min and y1_target <= y1_max:
            best_y1 = int(y1_target)
        else:
            if y1_target < y1_min:
                best_y1 = y1_min
            else:
                best_y1 = y1_max
        
        # Ensure we stay within bounds
        if best_x1 < 0:
            best_x1 = 0
        if best_x1 > n - l:
            best_x1 = n - l
        if best_y1 < 0:
            best_y1 = 0
        if best_y1 > m - w:
            best_y1 = m - w
            
        x2 = best_x1 + l
        y2 = best_y1 + w
        
        # Validate (x,y) is contained
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # Calculate distance squared to center
        center_x = (best_x1 + x2) / 2
        center_y = (best_y1 + y2) / 2
        dist_sq = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
        
        # If this is better, update best
        if best_rect is None or dist_sq < min_dist_squared:
            best_rect = (best_x1, best_y1, x2, y2)
            min_dist_squared = dist_sq
        elif dist_sq == min_dist_squared:
            # Lexicographically minimum
            current = (best_x1, best_y1, x2, y2)
            prev = best_rect
            if current < prev:
                best_rect = current
    
    # Special case: if no rectangle found, try smaller values
    if best_rect is None:
        # Try the simplest case: width = b_reduced, length = a_reduced
        w = b_reduced
        l = a_reduced
        
        if l <= n and w <= m:
            y1_min = max(0, y - w)
            y1_max = min(m - w, y)
            x1_min = max(0, x - l)
            x1_max = min(n - l, x)
            
            if y1_min <= y1_max and x1_min <= x1_max:
                # Find best x1, y1
                x1_target = 2 * x - l
                y1_target = 2 * y - w
                
                x1 = x1_min if x1_target < x1_min else x1_max if x1_target > x1_max else x1_target
                y1 = y1_min if y1_target < y1_min else y1_max if y1_target > y1_max else y1_target
                
                x1 = max(0, min(n - l, x1))
                y1 = max(0, min(m - w, y1))
                
                best_rect = (x1, y1, x1 + l, y1 + w)
    
    if best_rect is None:
        # Fallback: find any valid rectangle with ratio a:b
        # This should not happen given the constraints
        return (0, 0, a, b)
    
    return best_rect

print(*solve())
```

```python
def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Reduce the fraction a/b to lowest terms
    g = math.gcd(a, b)
    a_red = a // g
    b_red = b // g
    
    best_rect = None
    min_dist_squared = float('inf')
    
    # Try all possible widths that are multiples of b_red
    # Width w = k * b_red where k >= 1 and w <= m
    # Then length l = k * a_red
    
    for k in range(1, (m // b_red) + 1):
        w = k * b_red
        l = k * a_red
        
        # Check if length fits in x-direction
        if l > n:
            break
            
        # Find valid y1 range: y1 <= y <= y1 + w
        y1_min = max(0, y - w)
        y1_max = min(m - w, y)
        
        if y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # Find valid x1 range: x1 <= x <= x1 + l
        x1_min = max(0, x - l)
        x1_max = min(n - l, x)
        
        if x1_min > x1_max:
            continue
            
        # For this k, we have multiple valid rectangles
        # We need to find the one closest to (x,y) in Euclidean distance
        # Distance to center: (x - (x1+l)/2)^2 + (y - (y1+w)/2)^2
        
        # For fixed k, we want to minimize the distance
        # This happens when x1 is closest to (2x - l) and y1 is closest to (2y - w)
        
        x1_opt = (2 * x - l)
        y1_opt = (2 * y - w)
        
        # Choose x1 in [x1_min, x1_max] closest to x1_opt
        if x1_opt < x1_min:
            x1 = x1_min
        elif x1_opt > x1_max:
            x1 = x1_max
        else:
            x1 = int(x1_opt)
            
        # Choose y1 in [y1_min, y1_max] closest to y1_opt
        if y1_opt < y1_min:
            y1 = y1_min
        elif y1_opt > y1_max:
            y1 = y1_max
        else:
            y1 = int(y1_opt)
            
        # Clamp to bounds
        x1 = max(0, min(n - l, x1))
        y1 = max(0, min(m - w, y1))
        
        if x1 < 0 or x1 > n - l or y1 < 0 or y1 > m - w:
            continue
            
        x2 = x1 + l
        y2 = y1 + w
        
        # Calculate distance squared to (x,y)
        center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2
        center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2
        dist_sq = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
        
        # Update best rectangle
        if best_rect is None or dist_sq < min_dist_squared:
            best_rect = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
            min_dist_squared = dist_sq
        elif dist_sq == min_dist_squared:
            # Lexicographically minimum
            if (x1, y1, x2, y2) < best_rect:
                best_rect = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
    
    if best_rect is None:
        # Fallback - this should not happen
        # Try the smallest possible rectangle
        w = b_red
        l = a_red
        if w <= m and l <= n:
            y1 = y - w if y - w >= 0 else 0
            y1 = min(y1, m - w)
            x1 = x - l if x - l >= 0 else 0
            x1 = min(x1, n - l)
            if y1 >= 0 and y1 <= m - w and x1 >= 0 and x1 <= n - l:
                best_rect = (x1, y1, x1 + l, y1 + w)
    
    return best_rect

result = solve()
print(result[0], result[1], result[2], result[3])
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the given point (x,y)
   - Has exactly ratio a/b (width/height = a/b)
   - Is as close as possible to (x,y) in Euclidean distance
   - If ties, choose lexicographically smallest

## Key Insights

1. The sub-rectangle has dimensions (width, height) = (w, h) where w/h = a/b
2. We can express w = a*k and h = b*k for some positive integer k
3. The rectangle must contain (x,y), so:
   - x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ where x₂ - x₁ = w
   - y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ where y₂ - y₁ = h
4. We need to find valid values of k such that the constraints are satisfied

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Find valid range for k
For a given k:
- Width = a*k, Height = b*k
- x₁ can range from 0 to x (since x₁ ≤ x)
- x₂ = x₁ + a*k ≥ x, so x₁ ≥ x - a*k
- Similarly, y₁ can range from 0 to y, and y₂ = y₁ + b*k ≥ y, so y₁ ≥ y - b*k

### Step 2: Find valid constraint bounds
For a valid rectangle:
- x₁ ≥ max(0, x - a*k)
- x₁ ≤ min(x, n - a*k)
- y₁ ≥ max(0, y - b*k)
- y₁ ≤ min(y, m - b*k)

We need max(0, x - a*k) ≤ min(x, n - a*k) and max(0, y - b*k) ≤ min(y, m - b*k)

### Step 3: Find optimal k
We want the rectangle closest to (x,y). The center is at ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) = ((x₁+x₁+w)/2, (y₁+y₁+h)/2) = (x₁ + w/2, y₁ + h/2)

Distance from center to (x,y): 
d² = (x₁ + w/2 - x)² + (y₁ + h/2 - y)²

To minimize distance, we want x₁ ≈ x - w/2 and y₁ ≈ y - h/2.

### Step 4: Strategy
1. Find valid values of k (we need a*k ≤ n and b*k ≤ m, so k ≤ min(n/a, m/b))
2. For each valid k, find the optimal (x₁, y₁) that minimizes distance and satisfies constraints
3. Among all valid rectangles, pick the one with smallest distance, then lexicographically smallest

Let me implement this:

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Maximum possible k
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    best_distance_squared = float('inf')
    
    # Try all possible k values
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        w = a * k
        h = b * k
        
        # Calculate valid ranges for x1 and y1
        x1_min = max(0, x - w)  # x1 >= x - w to ensure x2 >= x
        x1_max = min(x, n - w)  # x1 <= x and x1 + w <= n
        
        y1_min = max(0, y - h)  # y1 >= y - h to ensure y2 >= y
        y1_max = min(y, m - h)  # y1 <= y and y1 + h <= m
        
        # Check if valid ranges exist
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # For this k, find optimal x1, y1 that minimizes distance
        # Center should be as close as possible to (x, y)
        # Center is at (x1 + w/2, y1 + h/2)
        
        # For fixed w, h, we want x1 + w/2 ≈ x, so x1 ≈ x - w/2
        # Similarly, y1 ≈ y - h/2
        
        x1_opt = int(round(x - w / 2))
        y1_opt = int(round(y - h / 2))
        
        # Clamp to valid ranges
        x1_candidate = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, x1_opt))
        y1_candidate = max(y1_min, min(y1_max, y1_opt))
        
        # Also check nearby values to ensure we find minimum
        # Check x1 in the neighborhood of the optimal value
        for dx in [-1, 0, 1]:
            for dy in [-1, 0, 1]:
                x1_test = x1_min + dx
                y1_test = y1_min + dy
                
                if x1_min <= x1_test <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_test <= y1_max:
                    x2 = x1_test + w
                    y2 = y1_test + h
                    
                    # Check if this rectangle is valid
                    if x2 <= n and y2 <= m:
                        center_x = (x1_test + x2) / 2.0
                        center_y = (y1_test + y2) / 2.0
                        dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                        
                        if (dist_sq < best_distance_squared or 
                            (dist_sq == best_distance_squared and 
                             (best_x1 is None or 
                              (x1_test, y1_test, x2, y2) < (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)))):
                            best_distance_squared = dist_sq
                            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_test, y1_test, x2, y2
    
    if best_x1 is None:
        # Fallback - should not happen given constraints
        # Find any valid rectangle
        w = a
        h = b
        x1_min = max(0, x - w)
        x1_max = min(x, n - w)
        y1_min = max(0, y - h)
        y1_max = min(y, m - h)
        
        if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
            x1 = x1_min
            y1 = y1_min
            x2 = x1 + w
            y2 = y1 + h
            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
```

```python
def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    best_distance_squared = float('inf')
    
    # Try all possible k values
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        w = a * k
        h = b * k
        
        # Calculate valid ranges for x1 and y1
        x1_min = max(0, x - w)
        x1_max = min(x, n - w)
        
        y1_min = max(0, y - h)
        y1_max = min(y, m - h)
        
        # Check if valid ranges exist
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # The optimal center is at (x, y), so we want the rectangle center to be as close as possible
        # Center is at (x1 + w/2, y1 + h/2)
        # We want to minimize (x1 + w/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + h/2 - y)^2
        
        # For x1, optimal value is x - w/2
        x1_opt = x - w / 2
        # For y1, optimal value is y - h/2
        y1_opt = y - h / 2
        
        # Try the closest integer values
        x1_candidates = []
        y1_candidates = []
        
        # Add the optimal value and nearby values
        for dx in [-1, 0, 1]:
            x1_candidate = int(round(x1_opt + dx))
            if x1_min <= x1_candidate <= x1_max:
                x1_candidates.append(x1_candidate)
        
        for dy in [-1, 0, 1]:
            y1_candidate = int(round(y1_opt + dy))
            if y1_min <= y1_candidate <= y1_max:
                y1_candidates.append(y1_candidate)
        
        # Try all combinations of candidates
        for x1 in x1_candidates:
            for y1 in y1_candidates:
                x2 = x1 + w
                y2 = y1 + h
                
                if x2 <= n and y2 <= m:
                    center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
                    center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
                    dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                    
                    # Check if this is better
                    if (dist_sq < best_distance_squared or 
                        (dist_sq == best_distance_squared and 
                         (best_x1 is None or 
                          (x1, y1, x2, y2) < (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)))):
                        best_distance_squared = dist_sq
                        best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that contains point (x,y) with length-width ratio exactly a:b
3. The sub-rectangle must have sides parallel to axes
4. We want to maximize the area (largest possible sub-rectangle)
5. If multiple solutions exist, we want the one closest to (x,y) in Euclidean distance
6. If still tied, we want lexicographically smallest (x1,y1,x2,y2)

## Key Insights

1. The ratio condition: (x2-x1)/(y2-y1) = a/b
2. This means (x2-x1) * b = (y2-y1) * a
3. So if we let width = w = x2-x1 and height = h = y2-y1, then w * b = h * a
4. Therefore, w = k * a and h = k * b for some positive integer k
5. We want to maximize the area, which means maximizing w * h = k² * a * b

## Solution Approach

1. For a given k, the width is k*a and height is k*b
2. The rectangle must contain (x,y), so:
   - x1 ≤ x ≤ x2 = x1 + k*a
   - y1 ≤ y ≤ y2 = y1 + k*b
3. This gives us constraints:
   - x1 ≤ x ≤ x1 + k*a → x1 ≥ x - k*a and x1 ≤ x
   - y1 ≤ y ≤ y1 + k*b → y1 ≥ y - k*b and y1 ≤ y
4. Also, x1 ≥ 0, y1 ≥ 0, x2 ≤ n, y2 ≤ m
5. So x1 ≤ min(x, n - k*a) and x1 ≥ max(0, x - k*a)
6. Similarly for y1

To maximize the area, we need to find the largest possible k such that there exists a valid rectangle.

## Step-by-step Algorithm

1. Find the maximum possible k
2. For the maximum k, find all possible rectangles that satisfy the constraints
3. Among those, find the one closest to (x,y)
4. If still tied, choose lexicographically smallest

```python
import sys
from math import sqrt

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # We want to maximize the area, so maximize k where width = k*a, height = k*b
    # Width constraints: 0 <= x1 <= x <= x1 + k*a <= n
    # Height constraints: 0 <= y1 <= y <= y1 + k*b <= m
    
    # Maximum possible k in x direction: k*a <= n and k*a <= x - 0 (x1 >= 0)
    # From x1 <= x <= x1 + k*a: x1 >= x - k*a and x1 <= x
    # So we need x - k*a >= 0 and x + k*a <= n
    # This gives: k*a <= x and k*a <= n - x
    
    # From y1 <= y <= y1 + k*b: y1 >= y - k*b and y1 <= y
    # So we need y - k*b >= 0 and y + k*b <= m
    # This gives: k*b <= y and k*b <= m - y
    
    # The maximum k is the minimum of:
    # k_max_x = min(x // a, (n - x) // a) 
    # k_max_y = min(y // b, (m - y) // b)
    # k_max = min(k_max_x, k_max_y)
    
    if a == 0 or b == 0:
        # This shouldn't happen given constraints
        print(0, 0, 0, 0)
        return
    
    # Find maximum possible k
    max_k_x = min(x // a, (n - x) // a) if a > 0 else float('inf')
    max_k_y = min(y // b, (m - y) // b) if b > 0 else float('inf')
    
    if max_k_x == float('inf') or max_k_y == float('inf'):
        # No valid rectangle exists
        print(0, 0, 0, 0)
        return
    
    max_k = min(max_k_x, max_k_y)
    
    if max_k == 0:
        # Only possible rectangle has zero size
        print(0, 0, 0, 0)
        return
    
    # For k = max_k, we have width = k*a, height = k*b
    # We need to find x1, y1 such that:
    # 0 <= x1 <= x <= x1 + k*a <= n
    # 0 <= y1 <= y <= y1 + k*b <= m
    
    # x1 >= max(0, x - k*a)
    # x1 <= min(x, n - k*a)
    
    # y1 >= max(0, y - k*b)
    # y1 <= min(y, m - k*b)
    
    x1_min = max(0, x - max_k * a)
    x1_max = min(x, n - max_k * a)
    
    y1_min = max(0, y - max_k * b)
    y1_max = min(y, m - max_k * b)
    
    # If no valid range, try smaller k
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        # Find the largest k such that there exists a valid rectangle
        # We need to find the maximum k where constraints are satisfied
        
        # Try k from max_k down to 0
        for k in range(max_k, -1, -1):
            width = k * a
            height = k * b
            
            # Check if valid dimensions
            if width > n or height > m:
                continue
                
            # Check x1 constraints
            x1_min_k = max(0, x - width)
            x1_max_k = min(x, n - width)
            
            # Check y1 constraints
            y1_min_k = max(0, y - height)
            y1_max_k = min(y, m - height)
            
            if x1_min_k <= x1_max_k and y1_min_k <= y1_max_k:
                max_k = k
                break
        
        if max_k == 0:
            print(0, 0, 0, 0)
            return
    
    # Now max_k is the largest possible
    width = max_k * a
    height = max_k * b
    
    # Find valid (x1, y1) pairs
    # We want the one closest to (x,y) in Euclidean distance
    # Distance: sqrt((x1 - x)^2 + (y1 - y)^2)
    
    # First, find all possible x1 and y1 values
    x1_list = list(range(x1_min, x1_max + 1))
    y1_list = list(range(y1_min, y1_max + 1))
    
    best_x1, best_y1 = None, None
    best_distance_squared = float('inf')
    
    for x1 in x1_list:
        for y1 in y1_list:
            x2 = x1 + width
            y2 = y1 + height
            
            # Check if this rectangle is valid (should be by construction)
            distance_squared = (x1 - x) ** 2 + (y1 - y) ** 2
            
            if distance_squared < best_distance_squared:
                best_distance_squared = distance_squared
                best_x1, best_y1 = x1, y1
            elif distance_squared == best_distance_squared:
                # Lexicographically smaller
                if (x1, y1) < (best_x1, best_y1):
                    best_x1, best_y1 = x1, y1
    
    # If no valid rectangle found
    if best_x1 is None:
        print(0, 0, 0, 0)
    else:
        print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x1 + width, best_y1 + height)
```

```python
def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # We need to find maximum k such that:
    # width = k * a, height = k * b
    # and we can place the rectangle so that it contains (x,y)
    
    # Constraints:
    # 0 <= x1 <= x <= x1 + k*a <= n
    # 0 <= y1 <= y <= y1 + k*b <= m
    
    # This gives:
    # x1 >= max(0, x - k*a)
    # x1 <= min(x, n - k*a)
    # y1 >= max(0, y - k*b)
    # y1 <= min(y, m - k*b)
    
    # So we need:
    # max(0, x - k*a) <= min(x, n - k*a) 
    # max(0, y - k*b) <= min(y, m - k*b)
    
    max_possible_k = 0
    
    # Try all possible values of k from 0 upwards until constraints fail
    k = 0
    while True:
        width = k * a
        height = k * b
        
        # Check if dimensions are valid
        if width > n or height > m:
            break
            
        # Check x constraints
        x1_min = max(0, x - width)
        x1_max = min(x, n - width)
        
        # Check y constraints  
        y1_min = max(0, y - height)
        y1_max = min(y, m - height)
        
        # Check if any valid position exists
        if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
            max_possible_k = k
        else:
            break
            
        k += 1
    
    if max_possible_k == 0:
        # No valid rectangle exists
        print(0, 0, 0, 0)
        return
    
    # Now we have the maximum possible k
    width = max_possible_k * a
    height = max_possible_k * b
    
    # Find the best (x1, y1) that minimizes distance and then lex order
    best_x1, best_y1 = None, None
    best_distance_squared = float('inf')
    
    x1_min = max(0, x - width)
    x1_max = min(x, n - width)
    
    y1_min = max(0, y - height)
    y1_max = min(y, m - height)
    
    # Enumerate all possible x1 and y1
    for x1 in range(x1_min, x1_max + 1):
        for y1 in range(y1_min, y1_max + 1):
            distance_squared = (x1 - x) ** 2 + (y1 - y) ** 2
            
            if distance_squared < best_distance_squared:
                best_distance_squared = distance_squared
                best_x1, best_y1 = x1, y1
            elif distance_squared == best_distance_squared:
                # Lexicographically smaller
                if (x1, y1) < (best_x1, best_y1):
                    best_x1, best_y1 = x1, y1
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x1 + width, best_y1 + height)
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the given point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a:b (where length = x₂-x₁, width = y₂-y₁)
   - Is as close as possible to point (x,y) in Euclidean distance
   - If multiple solutions, choose lexicographically smallest

## Mathematical Analysis

Given the ratio condition: (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b

This means: b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)

Let's denote:
- width = w = y₂ - y₁
- length = l = x₂ - x₁

So: l/w = a/b, which means l = (a/b)w

Since l and w must be integers, w must be a multiple of b, and l must be a multiple of a.

Let w = kb, then l = (a/b)(kb) = ka.

So we have: length = ka, width = kb for some positive integer k.

## Constraints

The rectangle must contain (x,y), so:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂

With x₂ = x₁ + ka and y₂ = y₁ + kb.

So:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + ka → x₁ ≥ x - ka and x₁ ≤ x
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + kb → y₁ ≥ y - kb and y₁ ≤ y

Also, we need:
- x₁ ≥ 0, x₂ = x₁ + ka ≤ n → x₁ ≤ n - ka
- y₁ ≥ 0, y₂ = y₁ + kb ≤ m → y₁ ≤ m - kb

## Finding k values

For a given k, we need to find valid x₁ and y₁:
- x₁ in [max(0, x - ka), min(x, n - ka)]
- y₁ in [max(0, y - kb), min(y, m - kb)]

We want to maximize the rectangle (or find the best one), and if multiple, minimize distance to center.

## Distance to center

The center of the rectangle is at ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) = ((x₁+x₁+ka)/2, (y₁+y₁+kb)/2) = (x₁ + ka/2, y₁ + kb/2)

Distance squared to (x,y): (x₁ + ka/2 - x)² + (y₁ + kb/2 - y)²

## Algorithm

1. For each possible k, check if valid rectangles exist
2. For each valid k, find all possible (x₁,y₁) pairs
3. Among all valid rectangles, choose the one with minimum distance to (x,y)
4. If tie, choose lexicographically smallest

But k can be very large (up to n or m). We need to find the optimal k.

Let me think about the range of k:

- From width constraint: kb ≤ m → k ≤ m/b
- From length constraint: ka ≤ n → k ≤ n/a

So k ≤ min(n/a, m/b)

Also, we need some valid space: we need x - ka ≥ 0 or x ≥ ka, and similarly for y.

## Key Insight

Since we're looking for maximum sub-rectangle, and k represents scaling, we want the largest possible k that allows a valid rectangle.

But actually, we want maximum sub-rectangle, which might mean we want to maximize area or the size. Since area is proportional to k², we want maximum k.

However, we also need to minimize distance to center. The problem says "maximum sub-rectangle" but then gives criteria for tie-breaking.

Looking at the examples more carefully:

Example 1: n=9,m=9,x=5,y=5,a=2,b=1
Ratio 2:1, so length:width = 2:1
So l = 2k, w = k

Constraints:
- x₁ ≤ 5 ≤ x₁ + 2k → x₁ ≥ 5-2k and x₁ ≤ 5
- y₁ ≤ 5 ≤ y₁ + k → y₁ ≥ 5-k and y₁ ≤ 5
- x₁ ≥ 0, x₁ + 2k ≤ 9 → x₁ ≤ 9-2k
- y₁ ≥ 0, y₁ + k ≤ 9 → y₁ ≤ 9-k

For k=1: 
- x₁ in [max(0,5-2), min(5,9-2)] = [3,5]
- y₁ in [max(0,5-1), min(5,9-1)] = [4,5]
- Possible rectangles: (3,4), (3,5), (4,4), (4,5), (5,4), (5,5)

For k=2:
- x₁ in [max(0,5-4), min(5,9-4)] = [1,5]
- y₁ in [max(0,5-2), min(5,9-2)] = [3,5]
- Possible rectangles: (1,3), (1,4), (1,5), (2,3), ..., (5,5)

For k=3:
- x₁ in [max(0,5-6), min(5,9-6)] = [0,3]
- y₁ in [max(0,5-3), min(5,9-3)] = [2,5]
- Possible rectangles

For k=4:
- x₁ in [max(0,5-8), min(5,9-8)] = [0,1]
- y₁ in [max(0,5-4), min(5,9-4)] = [1,5]

For k=5:
- x₁ in [max(0,5-10), min(5,9-10)] = [0,-1] → only [0] since upper bound -1
- y₁ in [max(0,5-5), min(5,9-5)] = [0,4]

Since x₁ ≤ min(5, 9-10) = min(5,-1) = -1, no valid x₁.

So k from 1 to 4.

We need to check distance for each valid rectangle.

Actually, the problem says "maximum sub-rectangle" - probably means maximum area, so maximum k.

So I'll try k from max(1, min(n//a, m//b)) down to 1, and for each k, find the best (x₁,y₁).

Wait, I need to be careful. "Maximum sub-rectangle" might mean maximum size, so we prioritize larger k.

But the problem also says "if there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x,y)".

So steps:
1. Find all valid k values in range [1, min(n//a, m//b)]
2. For each k, find all valid (x₁,y₁)
3. Among all rectangles, find the one with minimum distance to (x,y)
4. If tie, pick lexicographically smallest

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # The ratio is a:b, so length/width = a/b
    # length = ka, width = kb for some positive integer k
    # We need ka <= n, kb <= m, so k <= min(n//a, m//b)
    
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    best_rect = None
    best_dist_sq = float('inf')
    
    # Try all possible values of k from 1 to max_k
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        length = a * k
        width = b * k
        
        # Find valid x1 range
        x1_min = max(0, x - length)  # x1 <= x <= x1 + length
        x1_max = min(x, n - length)  # x1 <= x and x1 + length <= n
        
        # Find valid y1 range  
        y1_min = max(0, y - width)  # y1 <= y <= y1 + width
        y1_max = min(y, m - width)  # y1 <= y and y1 + width <= m
        
        # If no valid x1 or y1, skip this k
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # For each valid (x1, y1), calculate distance to center
        for x1 in range(x1_min, x1_max + 1):
            for y1 in range(y1_min, y1_max + 1):
                x2 = x1 + length
                y2 = y1 + width
                
                # Center of rectangle
                center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
                center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
                
                # Distance squared to (x,y)
                dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                
                # Check if this is better
                if (best_rect is None or 
                    dist_sq < best_dist_sq or 
                    (dist_sq == best_dist_sq and 
                     (x1, y1, x2, y2) < best_rect)):
                    best_dist_sq = dist_sq
                    best_rect = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
    
    print(best_rect[0], best_rect[1], best_rect[2], best_rect[3])

solve()
```

Wait, this might be too slow since k goes up to 10^9 and ranges could be large. We need to optimize.

We only need to check k values where valid rectangles exist. Also, for each k, we want the rectangle that minimizes distance to center.

For a fixed k, the center is at (x1 + length/2, y1 + width/2).

We want to minimize (x1 + length/2 - x)² + (y1 + width/2 - y)².

This is minimized when x1 and y1 are as close as possible to x - length/2 and y - width/2, respectively.

So for each k, the best x1 is:
- x1_opt = x - length/2 = x - a*k/2
- We need x1 in [x1_min, x1_max]

So we should choose x1 = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, round(x1_opt)))

Similarly for y1.

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # The ratio is a:b, so length/width = a/b
    # length = ka, width = kb for some positive integer k
    # We need ka <= n, kb <= m, so k <= min(n//a, m//b)
    
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    best_rect = None
    best_dist_sq = float('inf')
    
    # Only check k values where rectangles are possible
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        length = a * k
        width = b * k
        
        # Find valid x1 range
        x1_min = max(0, x - length)
        x1_max = min(x, n - length)
        
        # Find valid y1 range  
        y1_min = max(0, y - width)
        y1_max = min(y, m - width)
        
        # If no valid x1 or y1, skip this k
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # For this k, find x1 and y1 that minimize distance to center
        # Center is at (x1 + length/2, y1 + width/2)
        # We want to minimize (x1 + length/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + width/2 - y)^2
        
        # Optimal x1 would be x - length/2, constrained to [x1_min, x1_max]
        x1_opt = x - length / 2.0
        x1_target = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, int(round(x1_opt))))
        
        # We need to check the nearest values to x1_opt in the range
        # Since it's quadratic, the minimum is at the closest point
        x1_candidates = []
        if x1_opt >= x1_min and x1_opt <= x1_max:
            x1_candidates.append(int(round(x1_opt)))
            # Add nearby values if needed for lexicographic comparison
            if x1_opt - x1_min > 0:
                x1_candidates.append(x1_min)
            if x1_max - x1_opt > 0:
                x1_candidates.append(x1_max)
        else:
            x1_candidates.append(x1_min)
            x1_candidates.append(x1_max)
        
        # Similarly for y1
        y1_opt = y - width / 2.0
        y1_target = max(y1_min, min(y1_max, int(round(y1_opt))))
        
        y1_candidates = []
        if y1_opt >= y1_min and y1_opt <= y1_max:
            y1_candidates.append(int(round(y1_opt)))
            if y1_opt - y1_min > 0:
                y1_candidates.append(y1_min)
            if y1_max - y1_opt > 0:
                y1_candidates.append(y1_max)
        else:
            y1_candidates.append(y1_min)
            y1_candidates.append(y1_max)
        
        # Check all candidate combinations
        for x1 in x1_candidates:
            for y1 in y1_candidates:
                if x1 < x1_min or x1 > x1_max or y1 < y1_min or y1 > y1_max:
                    continue
                    
                x2 = x1 + length
                y2 = y1 + width
                
                # Check if within bounds
                if x2 > n or y2 > m:
                    continue
                    
                center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
                center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
                
                dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                
                # Check if this is better
                if (best_rect is None or 
                    dist_sq < best_dist_sq or 
                    (dist_sq == best_dist_sq and 
                     (x1, y1, x2, y2) < best_rect)):
                    best_dist_sq = dist_sq
                    best_rect = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
    
    print(best_rect[0], best_rect[1], best_rect[2], best_rect[3])

solve()
```

This is still potentially too slow. Let me simplify and just check if there's a better way.

Actually, for each k, the optimal x1 and y1 are those closest to x - a*k/2 and y - b*k/2. 

```python
def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    best_rect = None
    best_dist_sq = float('inf')
    
    # Try each k from 1 to max_k
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        length = a * k
        width = b * k
        
        # Valid ranges for x1 and y1
        x1_min = max(0, x - length)
        x1_max = min(x, n - length)
        y1_min = max(0, y - width)
        y1_max = min(y, m - width)
        
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # For this k, find best (x1,y1) to minimize distance
        # Center is at (x1 + length/2, y1 + width/2)
        
        # Target x1 that minimizes distance: x1 = x - length/2
        x1_target = x - length / 2.0
        x1 = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, int(round(x1_target))))
        
        # Check if the rounded value is in range, if not check boundaries
        x1_candidates = []
        if x1_min <= x1 <= x1_max:
            x1_candidates.append(x1)
        x1_candidates.append(x1_min)
        x1_candidates.append(x1_max)
        
        # Target y1 that minimizes distance: y1 = y - width/2
        y1_target = y - width / 2.0
        y1 = max(y1_min, min(y1_max, int(round(y1_target))))
        
        y1_candidates = []
        if y1_min <= y1 <= y1_max:
            y1_candidates.append(y1)
        y1_candidates.append(y1_min)
        y1_candidates.append(y1_max)
        
        # Check all combinations
        for x1_c in x1_candidates:
            for y1_c in y1_candidates:
                if x1_c < x1_min or x1_c > x1_max or y1_c < y1_min or y1_c > y1_max:
                    continue
                    
                x2 = x1_c + length
                y2 = y1_c + width
                
                center_x = (x1_c + x2) / 2.0
                center_y = (y1_c + y2) / 2.0
                
                dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                
                if (best_rect is None or 
                    dist_sq < best_dist_sq or 
                    (dist_sq == best_dist_sq and 
                     (x1_c, y1_c, x2, y2) < best_rect)):
                    best_dist_sq = dist_sq
                    best_rect = (x1_c, y1_c, x2, y2)
    
    print(best_rect[0], best_rect[1], best_rect[2], best_rect[3])

solve()
```

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

max_k = min(n // a, m // b)

best_rect = None
best_dist_sq = float('inf')

for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
    length = a * k
    width = b * k
    
    # Bounds for x1 and y1
    x1_low = max(0, x - length)
    x1_high = min(x, n - length)
    y1_low = max(0, y - width)
    y1_high = min(y, m - width)
    
    if x1_low > x1_high or y1_low > y1_high:
        continue
        
    # For this k, we want to minimize distance to (x,y)
    # Center at (x1 + length/2, y1 + width/2)
    
    # Optimal x1 is x - length/2, constrained to [x1_low, x1_high]
    best_x1 = None
    best_y1 = None
    
    # Check candidates around optimal position
    x1_candidates = [x1_low, x1_high]
    if x1_low <= x - length/2 <= x1_high:
        x1_candidates.append(int(round(x - length/2)))
    
    y1_candidates = [y1_low, y1_high]
    if y1_low <= y - width/2 <= y1_high:
        y1_candidates.append(int(round(y - width/2)))
    
    for x1 in x1_candidates:
        if x1 < x1_low or x1 > x1_high:
            continue
        for y1 in y1_candidates:
            if y1 < y1_low or y1 > y1_high:
                continue
                
            x2 = x1 + length
            y2 = y1 + width
            
            # Ensure within grid
            if x2 > n or y2 > m:
                continue
                
            center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
            center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2.0

                dist_sq < best_dist_sq or
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

Input
100 100 52 50 46 56

Output

Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the given point (x,y)
   - Has exact ratio (a/b) = (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁)
   - Is as close as possible to (x,y) in Euclidean distance to center
   - If multiple, choose lexicographically smallest

## Key Insights

1. The ratio constraint means: (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b
2. This implies: b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)
3. We can express this as: x₂-x₁ = k×a and y₂-y₁ = k×b for some positive integer k
4. The center of the rectangle is at ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2)
5. Distance from (x,y) to center should be minimized

## Solution Approach

1. For the rectangle to contain (x,y): we need x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ and y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂
2. Given width = k×a and height = k×b, we have:
   - x₁ = x - w/2 + offset (we need to find valid positions)
3. We need to find valid values of k such that the rectangle fits in bounds

Let me work through the constraints:

For a given k:
- Width = k×a, height = k×b
- We need: x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ and y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂
- So: x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k×a and y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + k×b

The center should be as close to (x,y) as possible.

## Implementation Strategy

1. Find all possible values of k such that the rectangle fits
2. For each valid k, find the rectangle that minimizes distance to center
3. Among those, pick the lexicographically smallest

Wait, I need to reconsider. Let me think about this more systematically:

The ratio constraint means: (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b
So: b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)

Let me define:
- width = x₂ - x₁ = k×a for some positive integer k
- height = y₂ - y₁ = k×b for some positive integer k

So the rectangle has dimensions k×a by k×b.

For this rectangle to contain (x,y):
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k×a
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + k×b

This gives us constraints:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k×a → x₁ ≥ x - k×a and x₁ ≤ x
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + k×b → y₁ ≥ y - k×b and y₁ ≤ y

Also, x₁ ≥ 0, y₁ ≥ 0, x₁ + k×a ≤ n, y₁ + k×b ≤ m

So x₁ ∈ [max(0, x - k×a), min(x, n - k×a)]
And y₁ ∈ [max(0, y - k×b), min(y, m - k×b)]

For each valid k, we can find if there's a solution, and among those, find the one closest to center.

The center is at ((x₁ + x₂)/2, (y₁ + y₂)/2) = ((x₁ + x₁ + k×a)/2, (y₁ + y₁ + k×b)/2) = (x₁ + k×a/2, y₁ + k×b/2)

Distance squared from (x,y) to center: (x - (x₁ + k×a/2))² + (y - (y₁ + k×b/2))²

To minimize distance, we want to minimize this expression.

For a fixed k, to minimize distance:
- x₁ should be as close as possible to x - k×a/2
- y₁ should be as close as possible to y - k×b/2

But x₁ is constrained by [max(0, x - k×a), min(x, n - k×a)]
And y₁ by [max(0, y - k×b), min(y, m - k×b)]

For each k that gives valid solutions, we calculate the optimal x₁, y₁, then check if it's feasible.

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # We need to find k such that the rectangle of size (k*a, k*b) fits
    # and contains (x,y)
    
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    min_dist_sq = float('inf')
    
    # Find the range of possible k values
    # k must be such that:
    # k*a <= n and k*b <= m, so k <= n/a and k <= m/b
    # k >= 1
    
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b) if a > 0 and b > 0 else 0
    
    # Also need to ensure we can contain (x,y)
    # x1 <= x <= x1 + k*a and y1 <= y <= y1 + k*b
    # So x1 >= x - k*a and x1 <= x
    # So we need x - k*a <= x, which is always true
    # and x1 >= 0, x1 + k*a <= n
    
    # k can be 1 to max_k
    
    # But also need to ensure we can place it to contain (x,y)
    # x1 >= max(0, x - k*a)
    # x1 <= min(x, n - k*a)
    # So we need max(0, x - k*a) <= min(x, n - k*a)
    
    # Similarly for y
    
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        width = k * a
        height = k * b
        
        # Check if we can place this rectangle to contain (x,y)
        # x1 must satisfy: max(0, x - width) <= x1 <= min(x, n - width)
        x1_min = max(0, x - width)
        x1_max = min(x, n - width)
        
        y1_min = max(0, y - height)
        y1_max = min(y, m - height)
        
        # If no valid position, skip
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # For this k, find the x1, y1 that minimizes distance to center
        # Center of rectangle: (x1 + width/2, y1 + height/2)
        # Distance squared: (x - (x1 + width/2))² + (y - (y1 + height/2))²
        
        # To minimize distance, we want x1 + width/2 as close as possible to x
        # So x1 should be as close as possible to x - width/2
        target_x1 = x - width / 2.0
        target_y1 = y - height / 2.0
        
        # Find the closest value in the valid range
        x1_opt = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, int(target_x1)))
        # But we need to be careful - we want the value that minimizes distance
        
        # Since we want minimum distance, and the range is continuous,
        # we choose x1 as close as possible to target_x1
        if target_x1 < x1_min:
            x1_opt = x1_min
        elif target_x1 > x1_max:
            x1_opt = x1_max
        else:
            x1_opt = int(target_x1)
            
        # Check if x1_opt is valid
        if x1_opt < x1_min or x1_opt > x1_max:
            x1_opt = x1_min if x1_min <= x1_max else x1_max
            
        y1_opt = max(y1_min, min(y1_max, int(target_y1)))
        if target_y1 < y1_min:
            y1_opt = y1_min
        elif target_y1 > y1_max:
            y1_opt = y1_max
        else:
            y1_opt = int(target_y1)
            
        if y1_opt < y1_min or y1_opt > y1_max:
            y1_opt = y1_min if y1_min <= y1_max else y1_max
            
        # Calculate distance squared
        center_x = x1_opt + width / 2.0
        center_y = y1_opt + height / 2.0
        dist_sq = (x - center_x)**2 + (y - center_y)**2
        
        # Check if this is better than current best
        if dist_sq < min_dist_sq or (dist_sq == min_dist_sq and (best_x1 is None or 
            (x1_opt, y1_opt, x1_opt + width, y1_opt + height) < (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2))):
            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_opt, y1_opt, x1_opt + width, y1_opt + height
            min_dist_sq = dist_sq
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

Input
100 100 52 50 46 56

Output

Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find a sub-rectangle in an n×m grid that:
1. Contains a given point (x, y)
2. Has length-width ratio exactly a:b (where length = x₂-x₁, width = y₂-y₁)
3. Maximizes the area (or equivalently, the dimensions)
4. If multiple solutions exist, choose the one closest to (x,y) in Euclidean distance
5. If still multiple, choose lexicographically minimum

## Key Insights

1. The ratio condition: (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b, which means (x₂-x₁) = k×a and (y₂-y₁) = k×b for some positive integer k.

2. To maximize the rectangle, we want the largest possible k such that:
   - x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂, and x₂ - x₁ = k×a
   - y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂, and y₂ - y₁ = k×b
   - 0 ≤ x₁ ≤ n, 0 ≤ y₁ ≤ m

3. For a given k, we need to find valid x₁, x₂, y₁, y₂:
   - x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k×a
   - y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + k×b
   - x₁ ≥ 0, x₂ = x₁ + k×a ≤ n
   - y₁ ≥ 0, y₂ = y₁ + k×b ≤ m

## Algorithm Steps

1. Find the maximum possible k such that we can fit a rectangle of dimensions k×a × k×b
2. For that maximum k, find valid positions that satisfy all constraints
3. Among valid positions, choose the one closest to (x,y)
4. If still tied, choose lexicographically minimum

## Finding Maximum k

For a rectangle of size (k×a) × (k×b):
- x₁ ≥ max(0, x - k×a)
- x₁ ≤ x
- x₂ = x₁ + k×a ≤ n, so x₁ ≤ n - k×a
- So x₁ ∈ [max(0, x - k×a), min(x, n - k×a)]

- y₁ ≥ max(0, y - k×b)
- y₁ ≤ y
- y₂ = y₁ + k×b ≤ m, so y₁ ≤ m - k×b
- So y₁ ∈ [max(0, y - k×b), min(y, m - k×b)]

For a valid k, we need:
- max(0, x - k×a) ≤ min(x, n - k×a)
- max(0, y - k×b) ≤ min(y, m - k×b)

- k ≤ n/a (from x dimension)
- k ≤ m/b (from y dimension)
- k ≤ x/a (from left constraint)
- k ≤ (n-x)/a (from right constraint)

5. y₂ = y₁ + k * b

It should be closest to (x,y) because x₁ and y₁ are chosen as close as possible to x and y respectively (by taking min with the constraints).

Algorithm:

1. Find maximum k = min(n // a, m // b)

Valid x₁ in [Lx, Rx] where:
- Lx = max(0, x - k*a)
- Rx = min(x, n - k*a)

Valid y₁ in [Ly, Ry] where:
- Ly = max(0, y - k*b)
- Ry = min(y, m - k*b)

We want to minimize distance² = (x₁ - x)² + (y₁ - y)²

This is equivalent to minimizing (x₁ - x)² + (y₁ - y)².

Since the function is convex and the domain is rectangular, the minimum occurs at one of the corners of the rectangle [Lx,Rx] × [Ly,Ry].

So we only need to check the 4 corners:
- (Lx, Ly)
- (Lx, Ry)
- (Rx, Ly)
- (Rx, Ry)

For each, compute distance² to (x,y), and pick the one with minimum distance².

If tie, choose lexicographically smallest (x₁, y₁).

Then compute x₂ = x₁ + k*a, y₂ = y₁ + k*b.

The function f(x₁,y₁) = (x₁-x)² + (y₁-y)² is convex, and on a rectangle, the minimum is at a vertex or at the point where the gradient is zero, but since it's increasing away from (x,y), the minimum on a bounded rectangle is at the closest point to (x,y).

For a function (x₁-x)² + (y₁-y)², the minimum over a rectangle occurs at:
- If (x,y) is in the rectangle, then at (x,y)
- Otherwise, at the closest boundary point.

In our case, (x,y) = (5,5), and the rectangle for (x₁,y₁) is [0,1] × [1,5].

(5,5) is not in this rectangle.

- x₁-direction: closest to 5 in [0,1] is 1
- y₁-direction: closest to 5 in [1,5] is 5

From (5,5) to (1,5): distance = 4

Why is (1,3) valid? It is, since y₁=3 in [1,5].

The distance from (x,y) = (5,5) to the center of a rectangle with bottom-left (x₁,y₁) is:
distance² = ((x₁+x₂)/2 - x)² + ((y₁+y₂)/2 - y)²
 = ((x₁ + (x₁ + k*a))/2 - x)² + ((y₁ + (y₁ + k*b))/2 - y)²
 = ((2x₁ + k*a)/2 - x)² + ((2y₁ + k*b)/2 - y)²
 = (x₁ + k*a/2 - x)² + (y₁ + k*b/2 - y)²

For fixed k, we want to minimize this over x₁ in [Lx,Rx], y₁ in [Ly,Ry].

This is a sum of two convex functions, so the minimum occurs at a boundary point.

The gradient of this function with respect to x₁ is 2(x₁ + k*a/2 - x), and with respect to y₁ is 2(y₁ + k*b/2 - y).

Setting to zero gives x₁ = x - k*a/2, y₁ = y - k*b/2.

We need to clamp to the boundaries.

So the optimal (x₁,y₁) is:
- x₁ = clamp(x - k*a/2, Lx, Rx)
- y₁ = clamp(y - k*b/2, Ly, Ry)

Then compute distance.

Example 1: k=4, a=2, b=1
x - k*a/2 = 5 - 4*2/2 = 5 - 4 = 1
y - k*b/2 = 5 - 4*1/2 = 5 - 2 = 3

Lx = max(0,5-8)=0, Rx = min(5,9-8)=1
Ly = max(0,5-4)=1, Ry = min(5,9-4)=5

So x₁ = clamp(1, 0,1) = 1
y₁ = clamp(3,1,5) = 3

This gives (1,3,9,7), exactly the expected output.

Perfect.

So algorithm:

1. k = min(n // a, m // b)
2. x₁_opt = x - k * a / 2
3. y₁_opt = y - k * b / 2
4. x₁ = clamp(x₁_opt, max(0, x - k*a), min(x, n - k*a))
5. y₁ = clamp(y₁_opt, max(0, y - k*b), min(y, m - k*b))
6. x₂ = x₁ + k * a
7. y₂ = y₁ + k * b

This gives the rectangle with maximum area (since maximum k), closest to (x,y), and if tie, lexicographically smallest (though in this case, the clamping should give the unique minimum for distance).

For lexicographical order: if there are multiple points with the same distance, we might need to choose lexicographically smallest.

But in this case, since the function is strictly convex, the minimum is unique, and there's only one point that minimizes distance.

So we just need to clamp to boundaries.

Edge cases: if the value is outside the range, clamp to nearest boundary.

In Python, we can use:
- if value < low: low
- if value > high: high
- else: value

- w = k * a' ≤ n

y1 in [max(0, y - h), min(y, m - h)] = [max(0, y - k*b'), min(y, m - k*b')]

Minimize over x1, y1 in their ranges.

The minimum occurs when x1 and y1 are as close as possible to x and y.

 distance to (x,y) = (x1 + w/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + h/2 - y)^2

 to minimize, we want x1 as close as possible to x - w/2, and y1 as close as possible to y - h/2.

 so both (17,8) and (18,8) have distance 0.25.

 if tie, choose lexicographically smallest, so (17,8) is smaller.

 then x2 = 17+69=86, y2 = 8+84=92

 so (17,8,86,92) matches expected.

 therefore, the algorithm is:

1. a, b = given
2. d = gcd(a,b)
3. a_prime = a // d
4. b_prime = b // d
5. k_max = min(n // a_prime, m // b_prime)
6. w = k_max * a_prime
7. h = k_max * b_prime
8. x1_opt = x - w/2
9. y1_opt = y - h/2
10. x1 = floor(x1_opt) if x1_opt >=0, else 0, and then clamp to [max(0, x - w), min(x, n - w)] — because from the containing condition, x1 must be in [max(0, x - w), min(x, n - w)] and also in [0, n-w]
11. similarly for y1 in [max(0, y - h), min(y, m - h)]

 but to be precise, x1 must be integer, so we need to find integer in the range that minimizes distance to x1_opt.

 specifically, we should consider the integer values around x1_opt, and choose the one that minimizes distance to x1_opt, with the range constraints.

 if x1_opt is in the range, choose x1 = round(x1_opt) or floor/ceil, but since distance is (x1 + w/2 - x)^2, it is minimized at the nearest integer to x1_opt.

 so x1 = round(x1_opt) if in range, else the boundary.

 similarly for y1.

 then if tie in distance, choose lexicographically smallest.

 in the example, x1_opt = 17.5, so round to 18, but 17 and 18 both have distance 0.25, and 17<18, so choose 17.

 so we should try both and choose the one with smaller distance, and if tie, the smaller value.

 in code, for a given range, we can:

- if x1_opt < Lx: x1 = Lx
- if x1_opt > Rx: x1 = Rx
- else: x1 = floor(x1_opt) or ceil, and we need to try both if in range and within distance, but since the function is convex, only the closest integer in the range matters, and if exactly at boundary, use that.

 for a point at 17.5, with range [0,31], both 17 and 18 are in range, and both have distance 0.25, so choose the smaller x1, which is 17.

 so in general, if x1_opt is in (Lx, Rx), then we can choose floor(x1_opt) and ceil(x1_opt), and choose the one that minimizes distance, and if tie, choose the smaller one.

 if x1_opt < Lx, then x1 = Lx
 if x1_opt > Rx, then x1 = Rx

 similarly for y1.

 in the example, x1_opt = 17.5 in (0,31), so try x1=17 and x1=18.

 distance at x1=17: (17 + 34.5 - 52)^2 = (51.5-52)^2 = 0.25
 at x1=18: (18+34.5-52) = (52.5-52)=0.5, (0.5)^2=0.25, same.

 so tie, choose smaller, so x1=17.

 then for y1: y1_opt = 8, in [0,16], so in range, try y1=8 and y1=8 (only one), so y1=8.

 then (17,8,86,92)

 for the first example: n=9, m=9, x=5, y=5, a=2, b=1

 d = gcd(2,1)=1
 a_prime=2, b_prime=1
 k_max = min(9//2, 9//1) = min(4,9) = 4
 w = 4*2 = 8, h = 4*1 = 4
 x1_opt = 5 - 8/2 = 5 - 4 = 1
 y1_opt = 5 - 4/2 = 5 - 2 = 3

 x1 in [max(0,5-8)=0, min(5,9-8)=1] = [0,1]
 y1 in [max(0,5-4)=1, min(5,9-4)=5] = [1,5]

 x1_opt = 1, in [0,1], so in range, try x1=1 (only since 0 and 1, 1 is the only one at 1, and 0 is at 0)

 distance at x1=1: (1+4-5)^2 = (0)^2 = 0
 at x1=0: (0+4-5)^2 = (-1)^2 = 1, so tie only at x1=1 with distance 0.

 so x1=1

 y1_opt = 3, in [1,5], try y1=3, distance (3+2-5)^2 = (0)^2 = 0
 at y1=2: (2+2-5)^2 = (-1)^2 = 1
 at y1=4: (4+2-5)^2 = (1)^2 = 1, so at y1=3, distance 0.

 so (1,3,9,7) matches expected.

 therefore, the algorithm is complete. 

 steps in code:

1. read n, m, x, y, a, b
2. d = gcd(a, b)
3. a_prime = a // d
4. b_prime = b // d
5. k_max = min(n // a_prime, m // b_prime)
6. w = k_max * a_prime
7. h = k_max * b_prime
8. x1_opt = x - w/2.0
9. y1_opt = y - h/2.0
10. Lx = max(0, x - w)
11. Rx = min(x, n - w)
12. Ly = max(0, y - h)
13. Ry = min(y, m - h)
14. x1 = find integer in [Lx, Rx] that minimizes (x1 + w/2 - x)^2, with tie-breaking to smaller x1
15. similarly for y1
16. x2 = x1 + w
17. y2 = y1 + h
18. output x1, y1, x2, y2

 for step 14, we can:

- if x1_opt < Lx: x1 = Lx
- elif x1_opt > Rx: x1 = Rx
- else: try floor and ceil of x1_opt, but only if in range, and choose the one with smaller distance, and if tie, choose the smaller value.

 in code, if x1_opt in [Lx, Rx], then let c1 = floor(x1_opt), c2 = ceil(x1_opt)
 if c1 and c2 in [Lx, Rx], then compute distances, choose the one with smaller distance, and if tie, choose smaller value
 if only c1 in range, then c1
 if only c2 in range, then c2

 similarly for y1.

 in the example with x1_opt=17.5, c1=17, c2=18, both in [0,31], distances both 0.25, so choose 17.

 in the first example, x1_opt=1, in [0,1], c1=1, c2=1, so only one, x1=1.

 in the second example for y1: y1_opt=8, in [0,16], c1=8, c2=8, so only one, y1=8.

 so it works. 

 let's code accordingly. 
Note: we must use integer arithmetic to avoid floating point errors, but since we need to compute w/2 and x - w/2, we can use floats or integers.

 to avoid floating point, we can work with the expression: (x1 + w/2 - x)^2 = (2*x1 + w - 2*x)^2 / 4

 so we can minimize (2*x1 + w - 2*x)^2, which is a integer.

 similarly for y1.

 so in code, for x1, we can compute the value of (2*x1 + w - 2*x) and minimize its square.

 since it is proportional, we can just minimize the absolute value of (2*x1 + w - 2*x), and if tie, choose smaller x1.

 because if |v1| = |v2|, then (v1)^2 = (v2)^2, and we choose the smaller x1.

 so algorithm for x1:

- if x1_opt < Lx: x1 = Lx
- elif x1_opt > Rx: x1 = Rx
- else: 
  - c1 = floor(x1_opt)
  - c2 = ceil(x1_opt)
  - if c1 >= Lx and c1 <= Rx: candidate1 = c1
  - if c2 >= Lx and c2 <= Rx: candidate2 = c2
  - if no candidate, use boundary (should not happen)
  - if only one candidate, use that
  - if two candidates, compute |2*c1 + w - 2*x| and |2*c2 + w - 2*x|, and choose the one with smaller absolute value, and if tie, choose the smaller x1.

 similarly for y1.

 in code, we can compute the value of the expression without floating point.

 let v1 = 2*c1 + w - 2*x
 let v2 = 2*c2 + w - 2*x

 then choose the one with smaller |v1|, and if tie, smaller c1.

 then x1 = that candidate.

 then x2 = x1 + w, etc.

 let's implement. 

 we need gcd and floor/ceil functions.

 in Python, from math import gcd

 code: 
```
import math

n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

d = math.gcd(a, b)
a_prime = a // d
b_prime = b // d

# maximum k
k_max = min(n // a_prime, m // b_prime)
w = k_max * a_prime
h = k_max * b_prime

# bounds for x1 and y1
Lx = max(0, x - w)
Rx = min(x, n - w)
Ly = max(0, y - h)
Ry = min(y, m - h)

# find x1
if w == 0 or w > n or h > m:  # should not happen
    # but if zero, then only at boundaries
    x1 = Lx
else:
    x1_opt = x - w / 2.0
    if x1_opt < Lx:
        x1 = Lx
    elif x1_opt > Rx:
        x1 = Rx
    else:
        c1 = int(math.floor(x1_opt))
        c2 = int(math.ceil(x1_opt))
        candidates = []
        if c1 >= Lx and c1 <= Rx:
            candidates.append(c1)
        if c2 >= Lx and c2 <= Rx:
            candidates.append(c2)
        if not candidates:
            x1 = Lx
        else:
            # choose the one with minimum |2*x1 + w - 2*x|, and if tie, minimum x1
            best_val = None
            best_x1 = None
            for candidate in candidates:
                val = abs(2 * candidate + w - 2 * x)
                if best_val is None or val < best_val or (val == best_val and candidate < best_x1):
                    best_val = val
                    best_x1 = candidate
            x1 = best_x1

# find y1
if h == 0 or h > m or w > n:  # should not happen
    y1 = Ly
else:
    y1_opt = y - h / 2.0
    if y1_opt < Ly:
        y1 = Ly
    elif y1_opt > Ry:
        y1 = Ry
    else:
        c1 = int(math.floor(y1_opt))
        c2 = int(math.ceil(y1_opt))
        candidates = []
        if c1 >= Ly and c1 <= Ry:
            candidates.append(c1)
        if c2 >= Ly and c2 <= Ry:
            candidates.append(c2)
        if not candidates:
            y1 = Ly
        else:
            best_val = None
            best_y1 = None
            for candidate in candidates:
                val = abs(2 * candidate + h - 2 * y)
                if best_val is None or val < best_val or (val == best_val and candidate < best_y1):
                    best_val = val
                    best_y1 = candidate
            y1 = best_y1

x2 = x1 + w
y2 = y1 + h

print(x1, y1, x2, y2)
```

 let me test with the examples.

 example 1: should output (1,3,9,7)

 in example 1: n=9, m=9, x=5, y=5, a=2, b=1
 d = gcd(2,1)=1
 a_prime=2, b_prime=1
 k_max = min(9//2, 9//1) = min(4,9)=4
 w=8, h=4
 Lx = max(0,5-8)=0, Rx = min(5,9-8)=1
 Ly = max(0,5-4)=1, Ry = min(5,9-4)=5

 x1_opt = 5 - 8/2 = 1
 1 in [0,1], so c1=1, c2=1, so only one, x1=1
 y1_opt = 5 - 4/2 = 3
 in [1,5], c1=3, c2=3, so only one, y1=3
 x2=1+8=9, y2=3+4=7 → (1,3,9,7) correct.

 example 2: n=100, m=100, x=52, y=50, a=46, b=56
 d = gcd(46,56)=2
 a_prime=46//2=23, b_prime=56//2=28
 k_max = min(100//23, 100//28) = min(4,3) = 3
 w=3*23=69, h=3*28=84
 Lx = max(0,52-69)=0, Rx = min(52,100-69)=31
 Ly = max(0,50-84)=0, Ry = min(50,100-84)=16

 x1_opt = 52 - 69/2 = 52 - 34.5 = 17.5
 in [0,31], so c1=17, c2=18
 both in [0,31], so candidates = [17,18]

 for c1=17: val = |2*17 + 69 - 2*52| = |34 +69 -104| = |103-104| = 1
 for c2=18: val = |36+69-104| = |105-104| = 1, tie
 so choose smaller x1, so x1=17

 for y1: y1_opt = 50 - 84/2 = 50-42=8
 in [0,16], c1=8, c2=8, so only one, y1=8

 so output (17,8,86,92) correct.

 therefore, the code is correct. 

 only edge case: if a and b are 0, but input says a,b>=1.

 and if w or h is 0, but a,b>=1, and k>=1, so w,h>=a_prime>=1, so not zero.

 also, the bounds should be non-empty because of the constraints on k.

Note: the problem has large numbers up to 10^9, but the operations are O(1), so it should be efficient. 
The only potential issue is floating point with large numbers, but for w/2, when w is large, x - w/2 might be large, but we are only using it for floor and ceil, and in the bounds, and the ranges are within the constraints. 

In Python, with integers up to 10^9, and we are doing only a few arithmetic operations, it should be fine. 

If we want to avoid floating point, we can work with the expression: we need to minimize |2*x1 + w - 2*x|, which is an integer, and we can compute it with integers.

For x1_opt, we can compute its floor and ceil with integer arithmetic: for a float x1_opt = x - w/2.0, we can do x1_opt = (2*x - w) / 2.0, and then floor and ceil.

In Python, with large numbers, might have floating point errors, so better to use integer arithmetic.

We can avoid floating point by: for the value of x1_opt, we use (2*x - w) / 2.0, but to get floor and ceil, we can use:

 floor_val = (2*x - w) // 2
 ceil_val = (2*x - w + 1) // 2 if (2*x - w) % 2 == 1 else (2*x - w) // 2

 but for floor and ceil of (2*x - w) / 2, it is (2*x - w) // 2 for floor, and (2*x - w + 1) // 2 for ceiling if (2*x - w) is odd, else (2*x - w) // 2.

 actually, floor of (2*x - w) / 2 is (2*x - w) // 2 if (2*x - w) is even or odd, in Python, for non-negative, // is floor, for negative, might be different.

 to be safe, we can use:

 floor_val = (2*x - w) // 2
 ceil_val = (2*x - w + 1) // 2

 in Python, for any integer, (n+1)//2 is ceiling of n/2 when n is integer? 

 for n=5: (5+1)//2 = 3, ceil(5/2)=3, yes
 for n=4: (4+1)//2 = 2, ceil(4/2)=2, yes
 for n=0:0, ceil(0)=0
 for n=-1: ceil(-0.5)=0, (-1+1)//2 =0, yes
 for n=-2: ceil(-1)= -1, (-2+1)//2 = -1//2 = -1, yes.

 so in general, ceil(n/2) = (n+1)//2 for any integer n in Python.

 floor(n/2) = n//2 in Python.

 so we can avoid floating point by:

 floor_val = (2*x - w) // 2
 ceil_val = (2*x - w + 1) // 2

 then we can work with integers.

 similarly for y1.

 then in the code, we can replace the floating point with integer arithmetic.

 for the bounds, we already have integers.

 so to avoid floating point, we can do:

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

Input
100 100 52 50 46 56

Output

Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates from (0,n) × (0,m)
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that contains point (x,y) with length-width ratio exactly a:b
3. The sub-rectangle must have sides parallel to axes
4. If multiple solutions exist, we want:
   - Closest to point (x,y) by Euclidean distance to center
   - Lexicographically minimum if still tied

## Key Insights

1. For a rectangle with width w = x₂-x₁ and height h = y₂-y₁, we need w/h = a/b, so w = k*a and h = k*b for some positive integer k.

2. Constraints:
   - x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ (so x₂ ≥ x and x₁ ≤ x)
   - y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ (so y₂ ≥ y and y₁ ≤ y)
   - x₁ ≥ 0, x₂ ≤ n, y₁ ≥ 0, y₂ ≤ m

3. For a given k, we have:
   - Width = k*a, so x₂ - x₁ = k*a
   - Height = k*b, so y₂ - y₁ = k*b

4. We can express the constraints as:
   - x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k*a
   - y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + k*b
   - x₁ ≥ 0, x₁ + k*a ≤ n
   - y₁ ≥ 0, y₁ + k*b ≤ m

5. From x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k*a, we get:
   - x₁ ≤ x
   - x₁ ≥ x - k*a

6. From y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + k*b, we get:
   - y₁ ≤ y
   - y₁ ≥ y - k*b

7. Constraints for x₁: max(0, x - k*a) ≤ x₁ ≤ min(x, n - k*a)
8. Constraints for y₁: max(0, y - k*b) ≤ y₁ ≤ min(y, m - k*b)

## Algorithm

1. For each possible value of k, check if a valid rectangle exists
2. Find the range of k values that are feasible
3. For each feasible k, find valid x₁ and y₁ values
4. Among all valid rectangles, choose the one closest to (x,y)
5. If still tied, choose lexicographically smallest

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # We need to find k such that:
    # width = k*a, height = k*b
    # x1 <= x <= x1 + k*a
    # y1 <= y <= y1 + k*b
    # x1 >= 0, x1 + k*a <= n
    # y1 >= 0, y1 + k*b <= m
    
    # Constraints on k:
    # k*a <= n (width constraint)
    # k*b <= m (height constraint)
    # x >= k*a (since x1 <= x and x1 + k*a >= x, need x1 >= x - k*a and x1 <= x)
    # x1 >= x - k*a >= 0, so x >= k*a when x - k*a >= 0
    # Actually: x1 >= max(0, x - k*a) and x1 <= min(x, n - k*a)
    
    # For k to be feasible:
    # k <= n // a (width constraint)
    # k <= m // b (height constraint)
    # x - k*a <= x (always true)
    # x - k*a >= -k*a (always true)
    # We need max(0, x - k*a) <= min(x, n - k*a)
    
    best_rect = None
    best_distance = float('inf')
    
    # Find the maximum possible k
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    # For each k from 1 to max_k, check if we can form a rectangle
    # Actually, k can be 0? No, because ratio a:b requires positive dimensions
    # So k >= 1
    
    # But we also need the rectangle to contain (x,y), so we need room
    # We can iterate through possible k values
    
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        # Width = k*a, Height = k*b
        width = k * a
        height = k * b
        
        # x1 must satisfy: max(0, x - width) <= x1 <= min(x, n - width)
        x1_min = max(0, x - width)
        x1_max = min(x, n - width)
        
        # y1 must satisfy: max(0, y - height) <= y1 <= min(y, m - height)
        y1_min = max(0, y - height)
        y1_max = min(y, m - height)
        
        # Check if valid range exists
        if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
            # Find the x1 and y1 that minimizes distance to (x,y)
            # Center of rectangle is at (x1 + width/2, y1 + height/2)
            # Distance squared = (x1 + width/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + height/2 - y)^2
            
            # We want to minimize this distance
            # For fixed k, we can find optimal x1 and y1
            
            # x1 in [x1_min, x1_max], we want to minimize |x1 + width/2 - x|
            # This is minimized when x1 is as close as possible to x - width/2
            
            x1_center = x - width / 2
            best_x1 = x1_min
            if x1_center > x1_max:
                best_x1 = x1_max
            elif x1_center < x1_min:
                best_x1 = x1_min
            else:
                # We can choose x1 such that it makes center as close as possible
                if x1_center - x1_min < x1_max - x1_center:
                    best_x1 = x1_min
                else:
                    best_x1 = x1_max
            
            # Actually, let's be more systematic
            # We want to minimize (x1 + width/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + height/2 - y)^2
            # For x1: we want to minimize |x1 + width/2 - x| = |x1 - (x - width/2)|
            
            x1_opt = x1_min
            if x1_max > x1_min:
                # Find the value that minimizes distance
                target_x1 = x - width / 2
                if target_x1 <= x1_min:
                    x1_opt = x1_min
                elif target_x1 >= x1_max:
                    x1_opt = x1_max
                else:
                    x1_opt = target_x1
            else:
                x1_opt = x1_min
            
            # For y1: minimize |y1 + height/2 - y|
            y1_opt = y1_min
            if y1_max > y1_min:
                target_y1 = y - height / 2
                if target_y1 <= y1_min:
                    y1_opt = y1_min
                elif target_y1 >= y1_max:
                    y1_opt = y1_max
                else:
                    y1_opt = target_y1
            else:
                y1_opt = y1_min
            
            # Calculate center and distance
            center_x = x1_opt + width / 2
            center_y = y1_opt + height / 2
            distance_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
            
            # Check if this is better than current best
            if best_rect is None or distance_sq < best_distance:
                best_distance = distance_sq
                best_rect = (x1_opt, y1_opt, x1_opt + width, y1_opt + height)
            elif distance_sq == best_distance:
                # Check lexicographical order
                current_rect = (x1_opt, y1_opt, x1_opt + width, y1_opt + height)
                best_rect = min(best_rect, current_rect)
    
    # If no rectangle found, we should have a valid one (since k>=1 and constraints exist)
    print(best_rect[0], best_rect[1], best_rect[2], best_rect[3])

solve()
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

100 100 52 50 46 56

Output
